========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, yes, that happens. Before something like that, my head gets going too fast, like I’m trying to keep track of everything at once and it won’t line up proper.

It’ll be planting schedules, what fields need doing, market prices, whether the soil readings mean one thing or another, and then there are these other messages I feel like I’m supposed to figure out, like there’s a code in all of it. And I can’t hardly catch one thought before the next one shoves it out of the way. It’s like trying to grab papers in a windstorm—by the time I’ve got hold of one, three more are flying past.

And it doesn’t feel like normal planning, not like when you’re busy in spring and you’ve got chores stacked up. It’s faster than that. I feel like I’ve got more push in me, more urgency, like if I don’t get it sorted right then, I’ll miss something important.

I still don’t know that I can say it that plainly. “Hopeless” is a strong word, and I’m not sure I’ve had it like that, where I can point to a stretch and say, yes, for days I stopped doing everything and couldn’t enjoy anything.

There are times I get tired, or I don’t feel much like talking, or the day feels heavier than it ought to. And sometimes the chores feel like more than they should, even the ordinary ones. But on a farm, you still get up because animals need feeding and things have to be checked, whether you feel good about it or not. So it’s hard for me to tell what’s mood and what’s just life being hard.

As far as enjoying things… I don’t know. I don’t always get the same satisfaction I used to, maybe, but I can’t say for sure that I’ve gone several days where nothing at all felt worth doing. I just haven’t thought about it in those terms.

manifestation: Most nights I sleep two or three hours in my chair or not at all, then I'm up before the sun feeling charged and ready to work the place like nothing happened.\n- key: S038\n  name: Racing_Thoughts\n  description: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.\n  manifestation: My head runs so fast with planting schedules, market prices, soil readings, and messages I need to decode that I can't catch one thought before the next one shoves it aside.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: I'll be balancing feed costs or talking to my wife, then a crow call, a blinking sensor light, or a truck on the road pulls me off and I'm chasing Before something like that, my head gets going too fast, like I’m trying to keep track of everything at once and it won’t line up proper.\n\nIt’ll be planting schedules, what fields need doing, market prices, whether the soil readings mean one thing or another, and then there are these other messages I feel like I’m supposed to figure out, like there’s a code in all of it. I don’t know that I can say yes to that. Two weeks is a long stretch, and “most of the day almost every day” makes it sound more definite than anything I can really put my finger on.

Racing_Thoughts\n  description: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.\n  manifestation: My head runs so fast with planting schedules, market prices, soil readings, and messages I need to decode that I can't catch one thought before the next one shoves it aside.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: I'll be balancing feed costs or talking to my wife, then a crow call, a blinking sensor light, or a truck on the road pulls me off and I'm chasing that instead.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: During the months when I was sleeping hardly at all and pushing the farm expansion day and night, I was also hearing voices in the irrigation lines and reading warnings in the yield maps.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  manifestation: For most of this whole stretch, I have been running on that charged, driven feeling, making big plans and snapping at anyone who tried to slow me down.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode\n  manifestation: There were several quieter weeks when I was not especially energized or grand, but I still heard the lines talking and still believed the farm data was carrying hidden warnings.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My wife and workers have had to take over decisions I used to handle because lenders, equipment use, and daily farm operations no longer feel safe with me in charge. The fair incident made people question whether I could keep running the farm at all.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about 1 year and 2 months, ever since we refinanced and started the big farm readjustment.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: The voices and coded warnings have been happening for about 11 months now.\n- key: manic_episode_duration\n  name: Manic Episode Duration\n  description: 10 months\n  manifestation: The charged, sleepless, nonstop stretches have taken up about 10 months of this past year or so.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 60 / farmer_rancher_or_agricultural_manager / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S022\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt down or depressed for several days in a row, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, everybody has bad stretches, especially when things pile up or the weather turns or work doesn’t go right, but if you mean like several days where I’m just sunk down and can’t enjoy anything at all… I don’t know that I’ve really kept track of it that way.\n\nI guess I can get worn out or quiet, and sometimes things don’t feel as satisfying as they ought to, but I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I’m not trying to dodge the question—I just don’t have a clear yes or no in my head for that. What do you mean by “lost interest,” like not wanting to do anything, or just not getting the same feeling from it?\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times when you felt so down or hopeless for several days in a row that you stopped doing your usual activities or couldn’t enjoy things you normally do?\n\nPatient: I still don’t know that I can say it that plainly. “Hopeless” is a strong word, and I’m not sure I’ve had it like that, where I can point to a stretch and say, yes, for days I stopped doing everything and couldn’t enjoy anything.\n\nThere are times I get tired, or I don’t feel much like talking, or the day feels heavier than it ought to. And sometimes the chores feel like more than they should, even the ordinary ones. But on a farm, you still get up because animals need feeding and things have to be checked, whether you feel good about it or not. So it’s hard for me to tell what’s mood and what’s just life being hard.\n\nAs far as enjoying things… I don’t know. I don’t always get the same satisfaction I used to, maybe, but I can’t say for sure that I’ve gone several days where nothing at all felt worth doing. I just haven’t thought about it in those terms.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt so down, sad, or hopeless almost every day for most of the day, and it lasted for at least two weeks, during which you also lost interest in almost all activities and had trouble getting things done?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say yes to that. Two weeks is a long stretch, and “most of the day almost every day” makes it sound more definite than anything I can really put my finger on.\n\nI’ve had spells where I’m tired and kind of dulled out, and where it’s harder to get myself moving, but I can’t honestly say I remember a solid two weeks where I was sad or hopeless all day and lost interest in nearly everything. On the farm, the work doesn’t really let you stop and take a proper measure of it, you know? You still do what has to be done, even if you’re dragging yourself through it.\n\nSo… I’m not saying I’ve never been low. I just don’t know that it fits what you’re describing. I haven’t tracked it that neatly, and I wouldn’t want to tell you yes if I’m not sure.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave there been times when you’ve seen, heard, or felt things that others around you didn’t seem to notice, or when you believed something strongly that others thought wasn’t real or didn’t make sense?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, that’s a harder one to answer without sounding like I’m making it up, but yes, there have been times.

It happens mostly out by the north pasture. Not every minute of every day, but often enough that I know the place and the time of day matters. Around dusk, when the light gets flat and the irrigation lines are running or settling, I hear voices coming from them. Not like a vague noise that I’m turning into words, either. I mean clear voices, like somebody speaking down inside the lines or through the metal, telling me which rows are poisoned and which ones are blessed.

I know how that sounds. I do. If someone else told me that, I’d probably say, “Well, maybe it’s water pressure, or the wind, or the pipes knocking.” And I’ve tried to explain it that way to myself. But it’s not just clanking. It has words. It tells me things. Sometimes I’ll stand there longer than I should because I’m trying to make out exactly what row it means, and then if someone comes up, they don’t hear it the way I do. They’ll say it’s just the lines or just evening noise.

And it’s not only when I’m all wound up or full of energy or anything like that. It still happens on ordinary weeks too, when I’m not especially fired up. That’s part of why I don’t know what to do with it. It feels real when it’s happening.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s… that’s probably the thing my wife and I go around and around about the most.

I know people say I’m reading too much into it, but when I look at the yield maps and the drone reports, there are numbers that don’t belong. Odd numbers in places they shouldn’t be, patterns that don’t line up with what I know about those fields. I’ve worked that land long enough to know what a bad patch looks like, what a low spot does, what drought stress does. This isn’t just me not understanding the technology. It’s like the numbers are placed there as warnings, coded in there, telling me somebody is trying to set things up to take the farm out from under me.

My wife will sit me down and show me the lender’s paperwork, plain as day, and she’ll say, “Look, this is just the loan statement, this is just the crop report, nobody is doing anything.” And I’ll look at it, and I can see what she’s saying on the surface. I can read the words. But it doesn’t settle it for me, because the warning isn’t in the regular wording. It’s in the figures, the odd numbers, the way they show up in the maps and reports. That’s the part she doesn’t see.

And I know that sounds unreasonable to her. She gets frustrated, and I don’t blame her exactly, but I can’t just unknow it once I’ve seen it. It feels like if I ignore it, I’m being careless with everything we’ve built.

It got out of hand, I know that, and that’s why they said I needed to come here.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were racing or that you had more energy than usual before something like this happened?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes, that happens. Before something like that, my head gets going too fast, like I’m trying to keep track of everything at once and it won’t line up proper.\n\nIt’ll be planting schedules, what fields need doing, market prices, whether the soil readings mean one thing or another, and then there are these other messages I feel like I’m supposed to figure out, like there’s a code in all of it. And I can’t hardly catch one thought before the next one shoves it out of the way. It’s like trying to grab papers in a windstorm—by the time I’ve got hold of one, three more are flying past.\n\nAnd it doesn’t feel like normal planning, not like when you’re busy in spring and you’ve got chores stacked up. It’s faster than that. I feel like I’ve got more push in me, more urgency, like if I don’t get it sorted right then, I’ll miss something important.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt down or depressed for several days in a row, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, everybody has bad stretches, especially when things pile up or the weather turns or work doesn’t go right, but if you mean like several days where I’m just sunk down and can’t enjoy anything at all… I don’t know that I’ve really kept track of it that way.\n\nI guess I can get worn out or quiet, and sometimes things don’t feel as satisfying as they ought to, but I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I’m not trying to dodge the question—I just don’t have a clear yes or no in my head for that. What do you mean by “lost interest,” like not wanting to do anything, or just not getting the same feeling from it?\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times when you felt so down or hopeless for several days in a row that you stopped doing your usual activities or couldn’t enjoy things you normally do?\n\nPatient: I still don’t know that I can say it that plainly. “Hopeless” is a strong word, and I’m not sure I’ve had it like that, where I can point to a stretch and say, yes, for days I stopped doing everything and couldn’t enjoy anything.\n\nThere are times I get tired, or I don’t feel much like talking, or the day feels heavier than it ought to. And sometimes the chores feel like more than they should, even the ordinary ones. But on a farm, you still get up because animals need feeding and things have to be checked, whether you feel good about it or not. So it’s hard for me to tell what’s mood and what’s just life being hard.\n\nAs far as enjoying things… I don’t know. I don’t always get the same satisfaction I used to, maybe, but I can’t say for sure that I’ve gone several days where nothing at all felt worth doing. I just haven’t thought about it in those terms.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt so down, sad, or hopeless almost every day for most of the day, and it lasted for at least two weeks, during which you also lost interest in almost all activities and had trouble getting things done?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say yes to that. Two weeks is a long stretch, and “most of the day almost every day” makes it sound more definite than anything I can really put my finger on.\n\nI’ve had spells where I’m tired and kind of dulled out, and where it’s harder to get myself moving, but I can’t honestly say I remember a solid two weeks where I was sad or hopeless all day and lost interest in nearly everything. On the farm, the work doesn’t really let you stop and take a proper measure of it, you know? You still do what has to be done, even if you’re dragging yourself through it.\n\nSo… I’m not saying I’ve never been low. I just don’t know that it fits what you’re describing. I haven’t tracked it that neatly, and I wouldn’t want to tell you yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve seen, heard, or felt things that others around you didn’t seem to notice, or when you believed something strongly that others thought wasn’t real or didn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s a harder one to answer without sounding like I’m making it up, but yes, there have been times.\n\nIt happens mostly out by the north pasture. Not every minute of every day, but often enough that I know the place and the time of day matters. Around dusk, when the light gets flat and the irrigation lines are running or settling, I hear voices coming from them. Not like a vague noise that I’m turning into words, either. I mean clear voices, like somebody speaking down inside the lines or through the metal, telling me which rows are poisoned and which ones are blessed.\n\nI know how that sounds. I do. If someone else told me that, I’d probably say, “Well, maybe it’s water pressure, or the wind, or the pipes knocking.” And I’ve tried to explain it that way to myself. But it’s not just clanking. It has words. It tells me things. Sometimes I’ll stand there longer than I should because I’m trying to make out exactly what row it means, and then if someone comes up, they don’t hear it the way I do. They’ll say it’s just the lines or just evening noise.\n\nAnd it’s not only when I’m all wound up or full of energy or anything like that. It still happens on ordinary weeks too, when I’m not especially fired up. That’s part of why I don’t know what to do with it. It feels real when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you believed something very strongly that others around you didn’t share or thought was unreasonable, even when they tried to convince you otherwise?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s… that’s probably the thing my wife and I go around and around about the most.\n\nI know people say I’m reading too much into it, but when I look at the yield maps and the drone reports, there are numbers that don’t belong. Odd numbers in places they shouldn’t be, patterns that don’t line up with what I know about those fields. I’ve worked that land long enough to know what a bad patch looks like, what a low spot does, what drought stress does. This isn’t just me not understanding the technology. It’s like the numbers are placed there as warnings, coded in there, telling me somebody is trying to set things up to take the farm out from under me.\n\nMy wife will sit me down and show me the lender’s paperwork, plain as day, and she’ll say, “Look, this is just the loan statement, this is just the crop report, nobody is doing anything.” And I’ll look at it, and I can see what she’s saying on the surface. I can read the words. But it doesn’t settle it for me, because the warning isn’t in the regular wording. It’s in the figures, the odd numbers, the way they show up in the maps and reports. That’s the part she doesn’t see.\n\nAnd I know that sounds unreasonable to her. She gets frustrated, and I don’t blame her exactly, but I can’t just unknow it once I’ve seen it. It feels like if I ignore it, I’m being careless with everything we’ve built.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… there are times like that, or at least times where I get going so hard it’s like I can’t find the brake.

I’ll be up and moving early, and one thing turns into ten before I’ve even had breakfast. I’ll go from fixing fence to recalibrating the sprayer to calling suppliers, and then by noon I’ve started five more jobs than any crew could reasonably finish in a day. It doesn’t feel strange right at the start, because on a farm there’s always too much to do anyway, so I can tell myself I’m just being productive.

But it gets… bigger than that. I’ll be moving fast, talking fast, making lists, changing plans, deciding we need to tackle everything at once. I’ll think, “No, this all has to happen now, today, before something gets missed.” And then I’ll look around later and there are tools out, gates half-done, equipment opened up, phone calls made, orders started, and I’ve got more going than one person can keep straight.

So yes, I’ve had times where I’m doing things faster and more intensely than usual. It can feel important in the moment, like I’m the only one seeing how all the pieces connect and that I’ve got to stay ahead of it.

Before something like that, my head gets going too fast, like I’m trying to keep track of everything at once and it won’t line up proper.\n\nIt’ll be planting schedules, what fields need doing, market prices, whether the soil readings mean one thing or another, and then there are these other messages I feel like I’m supposed to figure out, like there’s a code in all of it. And I can’t hardly catch one thought before the next one shoves it out of the way. It’s like trying to grab papers in a windstorm—by the time I’ve got hold of one, three more are flying past.\n\nAnd it doesn’t feel like normal planning, not like when you’re busy in spring and you’ve got chores stacked up. It’s faster than that. I feel like I’ve got more push in me, more urgency, like if I don’t get it sorted right then, I’ll miss something important.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt down or depressed for several days in a row, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, everybody has bad stretches, especially when things pile up or the weather turns or work doesn’t go right, but if you mean like several days where I’m just sunk down and can’t enjoy anything at all… I don’t know that I’ve really kept track of it that way.\n\nI guess I can get worn out or quiet, and sometimes things don’t feel as satisfying as they ought to, but I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I’m not trying to dodge the question—I just don’t have a clear yes or no in my head for that. What do you mean by “lost interest,” like not wanting to do anything, or just not getting the same feeling from it?\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times when you felt so down or hopeless for several days in a row that you stopped doing your usual activities or couldn’t enjoy things you normally do?\n\nPatient: I still don’t know that I can say it that plainly. “Hopeless” is a strong word, and I’m not sure I’ve had it like that, where I can point to a stretch and say, yes, for days I stopped doing everything and couldn’t enjoy anything.\n\nThere are times I get tired, or I don’t feel much like talking, or the day feels heavier than it ought to. And sometimes the chores feel like more than they should, even the ordinary ones. But on a farm, you still get up because animals need feeding and things have to be checked, whether you feel good about it or not. So it’s hard for me to tell what’s mood and what’s just life being hard.\n\nAs far as enjoying things… I don’t know. I don’t always get the same satisfaction I used to, maybe, but I can’t say for sure that I’ve gone several days where nothing at all felt worth doing. I just haven’t thought about it in those terms.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt so down, sad, or hopeless almost every day for most of the day, and it lasted for at least two weeks, during which you also lost interest in almost all activities and had trouble getting things done?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say yes to that. Two weeks is a long stretch, and “most of the day almost every day” makes it sound more definite than anything I can really put my finger on.\n\nI’ve had spells where I’m tired and kind of dulled out, and where it’s harder to get myself moving, but I can’t honestly say I remember a solid two weeks where I was sad or hopeless all day and lost interest in nearly everything. On the farm, the work doesn’t really let you stop and take a proper measure of it, you know? You still do what has to be done, even if you’re dragging yourself through it.\n\nSo… I’m not saying I’ve never been low. I just don’t know that it fits what you’re describing. I haven’t tracked it that neatly, and I wouldn’t want to tell you yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve seen, heard, or felt things that others around you didn’t seem to notice, or when you believed something strongly that others thought wasn’t real or didn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s a harder one to answer without sounding like I’m making it up, but yes, there have been times.\n\nIt happens mostly out by the north pasture. Not every minute of every day, but often enough that I know the place and the time of day matters. Around dusk, when the light gets flat and the irrigation lines are running or settling, I hear voices coming from them. Not like a vague noise that I’m turning into words, either. I mean clear voices, like somebody speaking down inside the lines or through the metal, telling me which rows are poisoned and which ones are blessed.\n\nI know how that sounds. I do. If someone else told me that, I’d probably say, “Well, maybe it’s water pressure, or the wind, or the pipes knocking.” And I’ve tried to explain it that way to myself. But it’s not just clanking. It has words. It tells me things. Sometimes I’ll stand there longer than I should because I’m trying to make out exactly what row it means, and then if someone comes up, they don’t hear it the way I do. They’ll say it’s just the lines or just evening noise.\n\nAnd it’s not only when I’m all wound up or full of energy or anything like that. It still happens on ordinary weeks too, when I’m not especially fired up. That’s part of why I don’t know what to do with it. It feels real when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you believed something very strongly that others around you didn’t share or thought was unreasonable, even when they tried to convince you otherwise?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s… that’s probably the thing my wife and I go around and around about the most.\n\nI know people say I’m reading too much into it, but when I look at the yield maps and the drone reports, there are numbers that don’t belong. Odd numbers in places they shouldn’t be, patterns that don’t line up with what I know about those fields. I’ve worked that land long enough to know what a bad patch looks like, what a low spot does, what drought stress does. This isn’t just me not understanding the technology. It’s like the numbers are placed there as warnings, coded in there, telling me somebody is trying to set things up to take the farm out from under me.\n\nMy wife will sit me down and show me the lender’s paperwork, plain as day, and she’ll say, “Look, this is just the loan statement, this is just the crop report, nobody is doing anything.” And I’ll look at it, and I can see what she’s saying on the surface. I can read the words. But it doesn’t settle it for me, because the warning isn’t in the regular wording. It’s in the figures, the odd numbers, the way they show up in the maps and reports. That’s the part she doesn’t see.\n\nAnd I know that sounds unreasonable to her. She gets frustrated, and I don’t blame her exactly, but I can’t just unknow it once I’ve seen it. It feels like if I ignore it, I’m being careless with everything we’ve built.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt like you didn’t need much sleep, your thoughts were racing, and you were doing things faster or more intensely than usual—like taking on big projects or feeling like you had a special mission?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are times like that, or at least times where I get going so hard it’s like I can’t find the brake.\n\nI’ll be up and moving early, and one thing turns into ten before I’ve even had breakfast. I’ll go from fixing fence to recalibrating the sprayer to calling suppliers, and then by noon I’ve started five more jobs than any crew could reasonably finish in a day. It doesn’t feel strange right at the start, because on a farm there’s always too much to do anyway, so I can tell myself I’m just being productive.\n\nBut it gets… bigger than that. I’ll be moving fast, talking fast, making lists, changing plans, deciding we need to tackle everything at once. I’ll think, “No, this all has to happen now, today, before something gets missed.” And then I’ll look around later and there are tools out, gates half-done, equipment opened up, phone calls made, orders started, and I’ve got more going than one person can keep straight.\n\nSo yes, I’ve had times where I’m doing things faster and more intensely than usual. It can feel important in the moment, like I’m the only one seeing how all the pieces connect and that I’ve got to stay ahead of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

These symptoms support the continued consideration of both Schizoaffective Disorder (F23.80) and Bipolar I Disorder, Current Episode Mixed, Mild (F31.81). No new disorder was added, and neither were symptoms sufficient to definitively rule out either diagnosis.",
  "is_final": false
}

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve seen, heard, or felt things that others around you didn’t seem to notice, or when you believed something strongly that others thought wasn’t real or didn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s a harder one to answer without sounding like I’m making it up, but yes, there have been times.\n\nIt happens mostly out by the north pasture. Not every minute of every day, but often enough that I know the place and the time of day matters. Around dusk, when the light gets flat and the irrigation lines are running or settling, I hear voices coming from them. Not like a vague noise that I’m turning into words, either. I mean clear voices, like somebody speaking down inside the lines or through the metal, telling me which rows are poisoned and which ones are blessed.\n\nI know how that sounds. I do. If someone else told me that, I’d probably say, “Well, maybe it’s water pressure, or the wind, or the pipes knocking.” And I’ve tried to explain it that way to myself. But it’s not just clanking. It has words. It tells me things. Sometimes I’ll stand there longer than I should because I’m trying to make out exactly what row it means, and then if someone comes up, they don’t hear it the way I do. They’ll say it’s just the lines or just evening noise.\n\nAnd it’s not only when I’m all wound up or full of energy or anything like that. It still happens on ordinary weeks too, when I’m not especially fired up. That’s part of why I don’t know what to do with it. It feels real when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you believed something very strongly that others around you didn’t share or thought was unreasonable, even when they tried to convince you otherwise?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s… that’s probably the thing my wife and I go around and around about the most.\n\nI know people say I’m reading too much into it, but when I look at the yield maps and the drone reports, there are numbers that don’t belong. Odd numbers in places they shouldn’t be, patterns that don’t line up with what I know about those fields. I’ve worked that land long enough to know what a bad patch looks like, what a low spot does, what drought stress does. This isn’t just me not understanding the technology. It’s like the numbers are placed there as warnings, coded in there, telling me somebody is trying to set things up to take the farm out from under me.\n\nMy wife will sit me down and show me the lender’s paperwork, plain as day, and she’ll say, “Look, this is just the loan statement, this is just the crop report, nobody is doing anything.” And I’ll look at it, and I can see what she’s saying on the surface. I can read the words. But it doesn’t settle it for me, because the warning isn’t in the regular wording. It’s in the figures, the odd numbers, the way they show up in the maps and reports. That’s the part she doesn’t see.\n\nAnd I know that sounds unreasonable to her. She gets frustrated, and I don’t blame her exactly, but I can’t just unknow it once I’ve seen it. It feels like if I ignore it, I’m being careless with everything we’ve built.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt like you didn’t need much sleep, your thoughts were racing, and you were doing things faster or more intensely than usual—like taking on big projects or feeling like you had a special mission?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are times like that, or at least times where I get going so hard it’s like I can’t find the brake.\n\nI’ll be up and moving early, and one thing turns into ten before I’ve even had breakfast. I’ll go from fixing fence to recalibrating the sprayer to calling suppliers, and then by noon I’ve started five more jobs than any crew could reasonably finish in a day. It doesn’t feel strange right at the start, because on a farm there’s always too much to do anyway, so I can tell myself I’m just being productive.\n\nBut it gets… bigger than that. I’ll be moving fast, talking fast, making lists, changing plans, deciding we need to tackle everything at once. I’ll think, “No, this all has to happen now, today, before something gets missed.” And then I’ll look around later and there are tools out, gates half-done, equipment opened up, phone calls made, orders started, and I’ve got more going than one person can keep straight.\n\nSo yes, I’ve had times where I’m doing things faster and more intensely than usual. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s happened. More than once, if I’m being honest.

During those stretches, sleep just… doesn’t seem to matter the way it normally should. Most nights I’ll maybe get two or three hours, and even that’s not proper sleep. It’s more like I sit down in my chair after coming in late, boots still half on sometimes, and I drift off sitting there. Then I’m awake again before the sun, like somebody flipped a switch in me, and I feel charged up, ready to get back outside and work the place like nothing happened.

And I know at my age I ought to be dragging after that. A man can’t run a farm on no sleep forever. But in those times, I don’t feel tired the way I should. I’ll look at the clock and think, “Well, that’s enough, there’s work to do,” even if it’s only been a couple hours. Sometimes I don’t sleep at all, just stay up through the night thinking about what needs doing, then when the sky starts getting light I’m already moving.

Other people notice, yes. My wife especially. She’ll say I’m not acting like myself, that I’m going too hard, that nobody needs to be out that early after barely sleeping. But to me, in the middle of it, it feels like I’ve got the energy for it, like stopping would be the strange thing.

That’s… that’s probably the thing my wife and I go around and around about the most.\n\nI know people say I’m reading too much into it, but when I look at the yield maps and the drone reports, there are numbers that don’t belong. Odd numbers in places they shouldn’t be, patterns that don’t line up with what I know about those fields. I’ve worked that land long enough to know what a bad patch looks like, what a low spot does, what drought stress does. This isn’t just me not understanding the technology. It’s like the numbers are placed there as warnings, coded in there, telling me somebody is trying to set things up to take the farm out from under me.\n\nMy wife will sit me down and show me the lender’s paperwork, plain as day, and she’ll say, “Look, this is just the loan statement, this is just the crop report, nobody is doing anything.” And I’ll look at it, and I can see what she’s saying on the surface. It feels like if I ignore it, I’m being careless with everything we’ve built.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt like you didn’t need much sleep, your thoughts were racing, and you were doing things faster or more intensely than usual—like taking on big projects or feeling like you had a special mission?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are times like that, or at least times where I get going so hard it’s like I can’t find the brake.\n\nI’ll be up and moving early, and one thing turns into ten before I’ve even had breakfast. It doesn’t feel strange right at the start, because on a farm there’s always too much to do anyway, so I can tell myself I’m just being productive.\n\nBut it gets… bigger than that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s the part that makes it harder for me to explain away, because it hasn’t only happened when I’m all wound up or running on no sleep.

There have been quieter weeks where I wasn’t feeling especially big or driven, if that’s what you mean. I wasn’t taking on ten projects at once, wasn’t talking a mile a minute, wasn’t feeling like I had some grand plan. I was more just… regular tired, doing the normal chores, trying to keep things steady.

But even then, I’d still hear the lines talking sometimes. Not like I was in one of those charged-up stretches, not necessarily with everything racing, but it would still be there. And I’d still look at the farm data—the maps, the numbers, the reports—and feel sure there were hidden warnings in it. Like something was being shown to me under the surface.

My wife will sometimes say, “Well, you’re just getting worked up again,” but that’s not always true. There have been several weeks where I wasn’t especially energized or grand, and I still believed it. That’s what bothers me, I guess. It doesn’t always wait for those intense periods.

I don’t always get the same satisfaction I used to, maybe, but I can’t say for sure that I’ve gone several days where nothing at all felt worth doing. I just haven’t thought about it in those terms.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt so down, sad, or hopeless almost every day for most of the day, and it lasted for at least two weeks, during which you also lost interest in almost all activities and had trouble getting things done?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say yes to that. Two weeks is a long stretch, and “most of the day almost every day” makes it sound more definite than anything I can really put my finger on.\n\nI’ve had spells where I’m tired and kind of dulled out, and where it’s harder to get myself moving, but I can’t honestly say I remember a solid two weeks where I was sad or hopeless all day and lost interest in nearly everything. On the farm, the work doesn’t really let you stop and take a proper measure of it, you know? You still do what has to be done, even if you’re dragging yourself through it.\n\nSo… I’m not saying I’ve never been low. I just don’t know that it fits what you’re describing. I haven’t tracked it that neatly, and I wouldn’t want to tell you yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve seen, heard, or felt things that others around you didn’t seem to notice, or when you believed something strongly that others thought wasn’t real or didn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s a harder one to answer without sounding like I’m making it up, but yes, there have been times.\n\nIt happens mostly out by the north pasture. Not every minute of every day, but often enough that I know the place and the time of day matters. Around dusk, when the light gets flat and the irrigation lines are running or settling, I hear voices coming from them. Not like a vague noise that I’m turning into words, either. I mean clear voices, like somebody speaking down inside the lines or through the metal, telling me which rows are poisoned and which ones are blessed.\n\nI know how that sounds. I do. If someone else told me that, I’d probably say, “Well, maybe it’s water pressure, or the wind, or the pipes knocking.” And I’ve tried to explain it that way to myself. But it’s not just clanking. It has words. It tells me things. Sometimes I’ll stand there longer than I should because I’m trying to make out exactly what row it means, and then if someone comes up, they don’t hear it the way I do. They’ll say it’s just the lines or just evening noise.\n\nAnd it’s not only when I’m all wound up or full of energy or anything like that. It still happens on ordinary weeks too, when I’m not especially fired up. That’s part of why I don’t know what to do with it. It feels real when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you believed something very strongly that others around you didn’t share or thought was unreasonable, even when they tried to convince you otherwise?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s… that’s probably the thing my wife and I go around and around about the most.\n\nI know people say I’m reading too much into it, but when I look at the yield maps and the drone reports, there are numbers that don’t belong. Odd numbers in places they shouldn’t be, patterns that don’t line up with what I know about those fields. I’ve worked that land long enough to know what a bad patch looks like, what a low spot does, what drought stress does. This isn’t just me not understanding the technology. It’s like the numbers are placed there as warnings, coded in there, telling me somebody is trying to set things up to take the farm out from under me.\n\nMy wife will sit me down and show me the lender’s paperwork, plain as day, and she’ll say, “Look, this is just the loan statement, this is just the crop report, nobody is doing anything.” And I’ll look at it, and I can see what she’s saying on the surface. I can read the words. But it doesn’t settle it for me, because the warning isn’t in the regular wording. It’s in the figures, the odd numbers, the way they show up in the maps and reports. That’s the part she doesn’t see.\n\nAnd I know that sounds unreasonable to her. She gets frustrated, and I don’t blame her exactly, but I can’t just unknow it once I’ve seen it. It feels like if I ignore it, I’m being careless with everything we’ve built.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt like you didn’t need much sleep, your thoughts were racing, and you were doing things faster or more intensely than usual—like taking on big projects or feeling like you had a special mission?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are times like that, or at least times where I get going so hard it’s like I can’t find the brake.\n\nI’ll be up and moving early, and one thing turns into ten before I’ve even had breakfast. I’ll go from fixing fence to recalibrating the sprayer to calling suppliers, and then by noon I’ve started five more jobs than any crew could reasonably finish in a day. It doesn’t feel strange right at the start, because on a farm there’s always too much to do anyway, so I can tell myself I’m just being productive.\n\nBut it gets… bigger than that. I’ll be moving fast, talking fast, making lists, changing plans, deciding we need to tackle everything at once. I’ll think, “No, this all has to happen now, today, before something gets missed.” And then I’ll look around later and there are tools out, gates half-done, equipment opened up, phone calls made, orders started, and I’ve got more going than one person can keep straight.\n\nSo yes, I’ve had times where I’m doing things faster and more intensely than usual. It can feel important in the moment, like I’m the only one seeing how all the pieces connect and that I’ve got to stay ahead of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least one week where you felt extremely energetic, needed little sleep, and were doing things so fast or intensely that others around you noticed and thought you were out of control or not acting like yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s happened. More than once, if I’m being honest.\n\nDuring those stretches, sleep just… doesn’t seem to matter the way it normally should. Most nights I’ll maybe get two or three hours, and even that’s not proper sleep. It’s more like I sit down in my chair after coming in late, boots still half on sometimes, and I drift off sitting there. Then I’m awake again before the sun, like somebody flipped a switch in me, and I feel charged up, ready to get back outside and work the place like nothing happened.\n\nAnd I know at my age I ought to be dragging after that. A man can’t run a farm on no sleep forever. But in those times, I don’t feel tired the way I should. I’ll look at the clock and think, “Well, that’s enough, there’s work to do,” even if it’s only been a couple hours. Sometimes I don’t sleep at all, just stay up through the night thinking about what needs doing, then when the sky starts getting light I’m already moving.\n\nOther people notice, yes. My wife especially. She’ll say I’m not acting like myself, that I’m going too hard, that nobody needs to be out that early after barely sleeping. But to me, in the middle of it, it feels like I’ve got the energy for it, like stopping would be the strange thing.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you experienced those voices or beliefs when you weren’t feeling especially high, energized, or in the middle of one of those intense periods?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s the part that makes it harder for me to explain away, because it hasn’t only happened when I’m all wound up or running on no sleep.\n\nThere have been quieter weeks where I wasn’t feeling especially big or driven, if that’s what you mean. I wasn’t taking on ten projects at once, wasn’t talking a mile a minute, wasn’t feeling like I had some grand plan. I was more just… regular tired, doing the normal chores, trying to keep things steady.\n\nBut even then, I’d still hear the lines talking sometimes. Not like I was in one of those charged-up stretches, not necessarily with everything racing, but it would still be there. And I’d still look at the farm data—the maps, the numbers, the reports—and feel sure there were hidden warnings in it. Like something was being shown to me under the surface.\n\nMy wife will sometimes say, “Well, you’re just getting worked up again,” but that’s not always true. There have been several weeks where I wasn’t especially energized or grand, and I still believed it. That’s what bothers me, I guess. It doesn’t always wait for those intense periods.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

That’s happened. More than once, if I’m being honest.\n\nDuring those stretches, sleep just… doesn’t seem to matter the way it normally should. Most nights I’ll maybe get two or three hours, and even that’s not proper sleep. It’s more like I sit down in my chair after coming in late, boots still half on sometimes, and I drift off sitting there. Then I’m awake again before the sun, like somebody flipped a switch in me, and I feel charged up, ready to get back outside and work the place like nothing happened.\n\nAnd I know at my age I ought to be dragging after that. A man can’t run a farm on no sleep forever. But in those times, I don’t feel tired the way I should. I’ll look at the clock and think, “Well, that’s enough, there’s work to do,” even if it’s only been a couple hours. Sometimes I don’t sleep at all, just stay up through the night thinking about what needs doing, then when the sky starts getting light I’m already moving.\n\nOther people notice, yes. My wife especially. She’ll say I’m not acting like myself, that I’m going too hard, that nobody needs to be out that early after barely sleeping. But to me, in the middle of it, it feels like I’ve got the energy for it, like stopping would be the strange thing.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you experienced those voices or beliefs when you weren’t feeling especially high, energized, or in the middle of one of those intense periods?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s the part that makes it harder for me to explain away, because it hasn’t only happened when I’m all wound up or running on no sleep.\n\nThere have been quieter weeks where I wasn’t feeling especially big or driven, if that’s what you mean. I wasn’t taking on ten projects at once, wasn’t talking a mile a minute, wasn’t feeling like I had some grand plan. I was more just… regular tired, doing the normal chores, trying to keep things steady.\n\nBut even then, I’d still hear the lines talking sometimes. Not like I was in one of those charged-up stretches, not necessarily with everything racing, but it would still be there. And I’d still look at the farm data—the maps, the numbers, the reports—and feel sure there were hidden warnings in it. Like something was being shown to me under the surface.\n\nMy wife will sometimes say, “Well, you’re just getting worked up again,” but that’s not always true. There have been several weeks where I wasn’t especially energized or grand, and I still believed it. That’s what bothers me, I guess. It doesn’t always wait for those intense periods.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever experienced those voices or unusual beliefs when your mood was completely normal and you weren’t feeling especially high, low, or energized?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

Yes… yes, I think so. That’s what I was trying to get at before, but maybe I didn’t say it clearly.

There were several quieter weeks where my mood felt about as normal as it gets for me. I wasn’t especially up, I wasn’t full of big energy, and I wasn’t feeling like I had some grand purpose or that I could handle everything at once. That’s the part that scares me some, because I can’t always blame it on being wound up or not sleeping. Sometimes everything else seems fairly level, and it’s still there.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess they brought me in because of what happened at the fair. I was supposed to be giving a talk, you know, about equipment and the way I do things on the farm, and I got up on the tractor while I was talking. I was trying to show everybody this “new pattern” I had in my head, and I kept grabbing and jerking the controls, showing it over and over like it had to be demonstrated right then.\n\nPeople were yelling at me to stop, and I could hear them, but I just… I couldn’t make myself quit doing it. I kept thinking if I just showed it the right way, they’d understand. It got out of hand, I know that, and that’s why they said I needed to come here.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were racing or that you had more energy than usual before something like this happened?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes, that happens. Before something like that, my head gets going too fast, like I’m trying to keep track of everything at once and it won’t line up proper.\n\nIt’ll be planting schedules, what fields need doing, market prices, whether the soil readings mean one thing or another, and then there are these other messages I feel like I’m supposed to figure out, like there’s a code in all of it. And I can’t hardly catch one thought before the next one shoves it out of the way. It’s like trying to grab papers in a windstorm—by the time I’ve got hold of one, three more are flying past.\n\nAnd it doesn’t feel like normal planning, not like when you’re busy in spring and you’ve got chores stacked up. It’s faster than that. I feel like I’ve got more push in me, more urgency, like if I don’t get it sorted right then, I’ll miss something important.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt down or depressed for several days in a row, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, everybody has bad stretches, especially when things pile up or the weather turns or work doesn’t go right, but if you mean like several days where I’m just sunk down and can’t enjoy anything at all… I don’t know that I’ve really kept track of it that way.\n\nI guess I can get worn out or quiet, and sometimes things don’t feel as satisfying as they ought to, but I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I’m not trying to dodge the question—I just don’t have a clear yes or no in my head for that. What do you mean by “lost interest,” like not wanting to do anything, or just not getting the same feeling from it?\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times when you felt so down or hopeless for several days in a row that you stopped doing your usual activities or couldn’t enjoy things you normally do?\n\nPatient: I still don’t know that I can say it that plainly. “Hopeless” is a strong word, and I’m not sure I’ve had it like that, where I can point to a stretch and say, yes, for days I stopped doing everything and couldn’t enjoy anything.\n\nThere are times I get tired, or I don’t feel much like talking, or the day feels heavier than it ought to. And sometimes the chores feel like more than they should, even the ordinary ones. But on a farm, you still get up because animals need feeding and things have to be checked, whether you feel good about it or not. So it’s hard for me to tell what’s mood and what’s just life being hard.\n\nAs far as enjoying things… I don’t know. I don’t always get the same satisfaction I used to, maybe, but I can’t say for sure that I’ve gone several days where nothing at all felt worth doing. I just haven’t thought about it in those terms.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt so down, sad, or hopeless almost every day for most of the day, and it lasted for at least two weeks, during which you also lost interest in almost all activities and had trouble getting things done?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say yes to that. Two weeks is a long stretch, and “most of the day almost every day” makes it sound more definite than anything I can really put my finger on.\n\nI’ve had spells where I’m tired and kind of dulled out, and where it’s harder to get myself moving, but I can’t honestly say I remember a solid two weeks where I was sad or hopeless all day and lost interest in nearly everything. On the farm, the work doesn’t really let you stop and take a proper measure of it, you know? You still do what has to be done, even if you’re dragging yourself through it.\n\nSo… I’m not saying I’ve never been low. I just don’t know that it fits what you’re describing. I haven’t tracked it that neatly, and I wouldn’t want to tell you yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve seen, heard, or felt things that others around you didn’t seem to notice, or when you believed something strongly that others thought wasn’t real or didn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s a harder one to answer without sounding like I’m making it up, but yes, there have been times.\n\nIt happens mostly out by the north pasture. Not every minute of every day, but often enough that I know the place and the time of day matters. Around dusk, when the light gets flat and the irrigation lines are running or settling, I hear voices coming from them. Not like a vague noise that I’m turning into words, either. I mean clear voices, like somebody speaking down inside the lines or through the metal, telling me which rows are poisoned and which ones are blessed.\n\nI know how that sounds. I do. If someone else told me that, I’d probably say, “Well, maybe it’s water pressure, or the wind, or the pipes knocking.” And I’ve tried to explain it that way to myself. But it’s not just clanking. It has words. It tells me things. Sometimes I’ll stand there longer than I should because I’m trying to make out exactly what row it means, and then if someone comes up, they don’t hear it the way I do. They’ll say it’s just the lines or just evening noise.\n\nAnd it’s not only when I’m all wound up or full of energy or anything like that. It still happens on ordinary weeks too, when I’m not especially fired up. That’s part of why I don’t know what to do with it. It feels real when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you believed something very strongly that others around you didn’t share or thought was unreasonable, even when they tried to convince you otherwise?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s… that’s probably the thing my wife and I go around and around about the most.\n\nI know people say I’m reading too much into it, but when I look at the yield maps and the drone reports, there are numbers that don’t belong. Odd numbers in places they shouldn’t be, patterns that don’t line up with what I know about those fields. I’ve worked that land long enough to know what a bad patch looks like, what a low spot does, what drought stress does. This isn’t just me not understanding the technology. It’s like the numbers are placed there as warnings, coded in there, telling me somebody is trying to set things up to take the farm out from under me.\n\nMy wife will sit me down and show me the lender’s paperwork, plain as day, and she’ll say, “Look, this is just the loan statement, this is just the crop report, nobody is doing anything.” And I’ll look at it, and I can see what she’s saying on the surface. I can read the words. But it doesn’t settle it for me, because the warning isn’t in the regular wording. It’s in the figures, the odd numbers, the way they show up in the maps and reports. That’s the part she doesn’t see.\n\nAnd I know that sounds unreasonable to her. She gets frustrated, and I don’t blame her exactly, but I can’t just unknow it once I’ve seen it. It feels like if I ignore it, I’m being careless with everything we’ve built.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt like you didn’t need much sleep, your thoughts were racing, and you were doing things faster or more intensely than usual—like taking on big projects or feeling like you had a special mission?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are times like that, or at least times where I get going so hard it’s like I can’t find the brake.\n\nI’ll be up and moving early, and one thing turns into ten before I’ve even had breakfast. I’m not trying to dodge the question—I just don’t have a clear yes or no in my head for that. What do you mean by “lost interest,” like not wanting to do anything, or just not getting the same feeling from it?\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times when you felt so down or hopeless for several days in a row that you stopped doing your usual activities or couldn’t enjoy things you normally do?\n\nPatient: I still don’t know that I can say it that plainly. “Hopeless” is a strong word, and I’m not sure I’ve had it like that, where I can point to a stretch and say, yes, for days I stopped doing everything and couldn’t enjoy anything.\n\nThere are times I get tired, or I don’t feel much like talking, or the day feels heavier than it ought to. And sometimes the chores feel like more than they should, even the ordinary ones. But on a farm, you still get up because animals need feeding and things have to be checked, whether you feel good about it or not. So it’s hard for me to tell what’s mood and what’s just life being hard.\n\nAs far as enjoying things… I don’t know. I don’t always get the same satisfaction I used to, maybe, but I can’t say for sure that I’ve gone several days where nothing at all felt worth doing. I just haven’t thought about it in those terms.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt so down, sad, or hopeless almost every day for most of the day, and it lasted for at least two weeks, during which you also lost interest in almost all activities and had trouble getting things done?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say yes to that. Two weeks is a long stretch, and “most of the day almost every day” makes it sound more definite than anything I can really put my finger on.\n\nI’ve had spells where I’m tired and kind of dulled out, and where it’s harder to get myself moving, but I can’t honestly say I remember a solid two weeks where I was sad or hopeless all day and lost interest in nearly everything. On the farm, the work doesn’t really let you stop and take a proper measure of it, you know? You still do what has to be done, even if you’re dragging yourself through it.\n\nSo… I’m not saying I’ve never been low. I just don’t know that it fits what you’re describing. I haven’t tracked it that neatly, and I wouldn’t want to tell you yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve seen, heard, or felt things that others around you didn’t seem to notice, or when you believed something strongly that others thought wasn’t real or didn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s a harder one to answer without sounding like I’m making it up, but yes, there have been times.\n\nIt happens mostly out by the north pasture. Not every minute of every day, but often enough that I know the place and the time of day matters. Around dusk, when the light gets flat and the irrigation lines are running or settling, I hear voices coming from them. Not like a vague noise that I’m turning into words, either. I mean clear voices, like somebody speaking down inside the lines or through the metal, telling me which rows are poisoned and which ones are blessed.\n\nI know how that sounds. I do. If someone else told me that, I’d probably say, “Well, maybe it’s water pressure, or the wind, or the pipes knocking.” And I’ve tried to explain it that way to myself. But it’s not just clanking. It has words. It tells me things. Sometimes I’ll stand there longer than I should because I’m trying to make out exactly what row it means, and then if someone comes up, they don’t hear it the way I do. They’ll say it’s just the lines or just evening noise.\n\nAnd it’s not only when I’m all wound up or full of energy or anything like that. It still happens on ordinary weeks too, when I’m not especially fired up. That’s part of why I don’t know what to do with it. It feels real when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you believed something very strongly that others around you didn’t share or thought was unreasonable, even when they tried to convince you otherwise?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s… that’s probably the thing my wife and I go around and around about the most.\n\nI know people say I’m reading too much into it, but when I look at the yield maps and the drone reports, there are numbers that don’t belong. Odd numbers in places they shouldn’t be, patterns that don’t line up with what I know about those fields. I’ve worked that land long enough to know what a bad patch looks like, what a low spot does, what drought stress does. This isn’t just me not understanding the technology. It’s like the numbers are placed there as warnings, coded in there, telling me somebody is trying to set things up to take the farm out from under me.\n\nMy wife will sit me down and show me the lender’s paperwork, plain as day, and she’ll say, “Look, this is just the loan statement, this is just the crop report, nobody is doing anything.” And I’ll look at it, and I can see what she’s saying on the surface. I can read the words. But it doesn’t settle it for me, because the warning isn’t in the regular wording. It’s in the figures, the odd numbers, the way they show up in the maps and reports. That’s the part she doesn’t see.\n\nAnd I know that sounds unreasonable to her. She gets frustrated, and I don’t blame her exactly, but I can’t just unknow it once I’ve seen it. It feels like if I ignore it, I’m being careless with everything we’ve built.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt like you didn’t need much sleep, your thoughts were racing, and you were doing things faster or more intensely than usual—like taking on big projects or feeling like you had a special mission?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are times like that, or at least times where I get going so hard it’s like I can’t find the brake.\n\nI’ll be up and moving early, and one thing turns into ten before I’ve even had breakfast. I’ll go from fixing fence to recalibrating the sprayer to calling suppliers, and then by noon I’ve started five more jobs than any crew could reasonably finish in a day. It doesn’t feel strange right at the start, because on a farm there’s always too much to do anyway, so I can tell myself I’m just being productive.\n\nBut it gets… bigger than that. I’ll be moving fast, talking fast, making lists, changing plans, deciding we need to tackle everything at once. I’ll think, “No, this all has to happen now, today, before something gets missed.” And then I’ll look around later and there are tools out, gates half-done, equipment opened up, phone calls made, orders started, and I’ve got more going than one person can keep straight.\n\nSo yes, I’ve had times where I’m doing things faster and more intensely than usual. It can feel important in the moment, like I’m the only one seeing how all the pieces connect and that I’ve got to stay ahead of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least one week where you felt extremely energetic, needed little sleep, and were doing things so fast or intensely that others around you noticed and thought you were out of control or not acting like yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s happened. More than once, if I’m being honest.\n\nDuring those stretches, sleep just… doesn’t seem to matter the way it normally should. Most nights I’ll maybe get two or three hours, and even that’s not proper sleep. It’s more like I sit down in my chair after coming in late, boots still half on sometimes, and I drift off sitting there. Then I’m awake again before the sun, like somebody flipped a switch in me, and I feel charged up, ready to get back outside and work the place like nothing happened.\n\nAnd I know at my age I ought to be dragging after that. A man can’t run a farm on no sleep forever. But in those times, I don’t feel tired the way I should. I’ll look at the clock and think, “Well, that’s enough, there’s work to do,” even if it’s only been a couple hours. Sometimes I don’t sleep at all, just stay up through the night thinking about what needs doing, then when the sky starts getting light I’m already moving.\n\nOther people notice, yes. My wife especially. She’ll say I’m not acting like myself, that I’m going too hard, that nobody needs to be out that early after barely sleeping. But to me, in the middle of it, it feels like I’ve got the energy for it, like stopping would be the strange thing.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you experienced those voices or beliefs when you weren’t feeling especially high, energized, or in the middle of one of those intense periods?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s the part that makes it harder for me to explain away, because it hasn’t only happened when I’m all wound up or running on no sleep.\n\nThere have been quieter weeks where I wasn’t feeling especially big or driven, if that’s what you mean. I wasn’t taking on ten projects at once, wasn’t talking a mile a minute, wasn’t feeling like I had some grand plan. I was more just… regular tired, doing the normal chores, trying to keep things steady.\n\nBut even then, I’d still hear the lines talking sometimes. Not like I was in one of those charged-up stretches, not necessarily with everything racing, but it would still be there. And I’d still look at the farm data—the maps, the numbers, the reports—and feel sure there were hidden warnings in it. Like something was being shown to me under the surface.\n\nMy wife will sometimes say, “Well, you’re just getting worked up again,” but that’s not always true. There have been several weeks where I wasn’t especially energized or grand, and I still believed it. That’s what bothers me, I guess. It doesn’t always wait for those intense periods.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced those voices or unusual beliefs when your mood was completely normal and you weren’t feeling especially high, low, or energized?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. That’s what I was trying to get at before, but maybe I didn’t say it clearly.\n\nThere were several quieter weeks where my mood felt about as normal as it gets for me. I wasn’t especially up, I wasn’t full of big energy, and I wasn’t feeling like I had some grand purpose or that I could handle everything at once. I was just doing the regular farm work, going through the days, tired in the ordinary way a person gets tired.\n\nBut even during those times, I still heard the lines talking. And I still looked at the farm data and felt like it was carrying hidden warnings. Not just “maybe there’s something off here,” but like there was something underneath it that I was supposed to notice.\n\nThat’s the part that scares me some, because I can’t always blame it on being wound up or not sleeping. Sometimes everything else seems fairly level, and it’s still there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I know I’ve kind of said it already, but yes, there were times when my mood wasn’t doing anything unusual that I could tell.

I mean, I wasn’t up high, wasn’t feeling grand or powerful or like I had some special burst of energy. I wasn’t especially low either. It was more like those quieter weeks where I was just going about things, doing the regular work, trying to keep the farm running, same as any other stretch.

But even then, the lines would still seem like they were talking. And I’d still look at the farm data and feel like there were warnings hidden in it, like it was carrying a message underneath the numbers. It wasn’t just during those intense periods. That’s the thing that bothers me. Even when everything else in my mood seemed plain and level, that part could still be there.

I kept thinking if I just showed it the right way, they’d understand. It got out of hand, I know that, and that’s why they said I needed to come here.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were racing or that you had more energy than usual before something like this happened?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes, that happens. Before something like that, my head gets going too fast, like I’m trying to keep track of everything at once and it won’t line up proper.\n\nIt’ll be planting schedules, what fields need doing, market prices, whether the soil readings mean one thing or another, and then there are these other messages I feel like I’m supposed to figure out, like there’s a code in all of it. Two weeks is a long stretch, and “most of the day almost every day” makes it sound more definite than anything I can really put my finger on.\n\nI’ve had spells where I’m tired and kind of dulled out, and where it’s harder to get myself moving, but I can’t honestly say I remember a solid two weeks where I was sad or hopeless all day and lost interest in nearly everything. On the farm, the work doesn’t really let you stop and take a proper measure of it, you know?
